EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
7.8 Facilities and Services 
Maintenance
The City of Oceanside employs a variety of fund-
ing mechanisms and program in order to main-
tain municipal facilities and public services.
LANDSCAPE MAINTENANCE DISTRICTS 
(LMDS) 
Landscape Maintenance Districts (LMDs) are 
special districts formed to provide benefiting 
private property owners the opportunity to 
pay for enhanced landscaping and appurtenant 
improvements, maintenance, and services. The 
City’s landscape contractor provides maintenance 
to approximately 250 acres of landscape mainte-
nance districts (LMDs) throughout the City. 
The City’s eleven LMDs were established pursu-
ant to the authority granted by the Landscaping 
and Lighting Act of 1972 for the California Streets 
and Highways Code. This legislation provided 
the means to finance the maintenance of land-
scape improvements within the boundaries of 
each district. The amount of the assessment 
levied against a parcel is based on benefit unit 
costs. %enefit units are assigned to each parcel 
in accordance with its land use designation and 
proximity to district landscaping. Each district’s 
CALIFORNIA SURF MUSEUM
The California Surf Museum was founded in 1986 
to showcase the international history of surfing. 
Consisting of both permanent and revolving 
exhibits, the museum’s collections represent 
one of the most comprehensive collections of 
surfing history. 0useum materials include surf-
boards, surf art and equipment, photographs, 
magazines, and video. The museum is visited by 
approximately 20,000 visitors per year.
In 1995, the City of Oceanside offered the 
California Surf Museum a space at the corner 
of North Coast Highway and Pier View Way, 
where it stayed until 2009. in 2006, the City of 
2ceanside offered the museum a new and larger 
space at 312 Pier View Way. in 2009, the museum 
moved into the renovated facility. 
In addition to its exhibits, the museum has 
hosted a variety of events including book sign-
ings, events honoring luminaries of the surfing 
community, surf film festival, and an annual gala. 
in 2007, the museum drew upon its decades-
long collection of stories and images to publish 
a photo book, Surfing in San Diego, the first 
attempt to document the history of surfing in 
the county. in 2011, the California Surf Museum 
was awarded the Museum Assessment grant by 
the American Association of Museums. 


total costs, divided by the total benefit units in 
the district, determine the assessment per ben-
efit unit. 0ost single-family homes are assessed 
one benefit unit. An additional .1 benefit unit is 
assessed if the single-family home is adjacent to 
district landscaping. Condominiums and apart-
ments are assigned . benefit unit per dwelling. 
Assessments on the benefitting properties are 
collected by the San Diego County Assessor’s 
office through property tax bills. 
Assessment levels were set at the time of for-
mation. The LMDs were formed with the inten-
tion of being self-supporting, but many were 
formed prior to the passage of Proposition 
218, the “Right to Vote on Taxes Act”, in 1996. 
Among other things, Proposition 218 amended 
the California Constitution to include a provi-
sion which precludes Districts from increasing 
assessments beyond maximum levels without 
a special ballot process and the majority vote of 
property owners. Special balloting procedures 
may be implemented to give District participants 
the opportunity to raise the maximum allowable 
assessment to cover the district’s maintenance 
needs. Over the years, costs to support the 
Districts have steadily risen, but many of the 
LMDs have not supported ballot measures to 
increase assessment levels.  Of the eleven LMDs, 
only four have a Consumer Price Index (CPI) esca-
lator that adjusts assessment levels year over 
year. These Districts are: Douglas Park (includ-
ing the Vandegrift annexation), Guajome Ridge, 
Rancho Hermosa, and Vista del Rio. Vista del Rio 
was formed with a built-in CPI escalator, while 
the other three Districts successfully passed 
ballot measures to implement one. However, 
the remaining seven Districts (Del Oro Hills, 
Marlado Highlands, Mission Meadows, Peacock 
Hills, Santa Fe Mesa, Sunburst Homes, and 
Sunset Hills) do not have built in CPI adjustment 
escalators. Many of the previous ballot mea-
sures have been unsuccessful.  Subsequently, 
budgetary shortfalls have been addressed by 
using a combination of assessments and reserve 
funds to meet operating needs. Reserve funding 
is being depleted, and service levels in some of 
the underfunded Districts have been reduced to 
compensate for the rising costs of maintenance 
and flat assessments collected.

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
PROPOSITION H
in -une , 2ceanside voters overwhelm-
ingly approved Proposition H, authorizing the 
Oceanside Unified School District to issue up 
to $195 million in general obligation bonds for 
school facility improvements, enabling the dis-
trict to complete the repairs and modernization 
effort established under the Proposition * bond 
construction program to provide high quality 
facilities to all Oceanside students.
The schools will receive repairs, modernization, 
upgrades, expansion, and new construction 
as warranted by enrollment and facility usage 
needs to provide equitable facilities for all 
students.
MEASURE X
in 2018, Measure X proposed to raise the sales 
tax in Oceanside by a half-cent for a period of 
seven years to fund public safety programs, 
infrastructure improvements, road repairs, and 
aid for homelessness. This increase was initiated 
on April 1, 2019, bringing Oceanside’s sales tax 
rate to .5 percent. 0easure ; will be effective 
for seven years.  in Year One, the Measure X 
infrastructure-related budget included:
• Beachfront improvements, including a 
police substation, public restrooms, garage 
and storage space for beach operations, a 
new public plaza area, upgraded stairways, 
pathways, and a sewer lift station;
• Rehabilitation of the pier to meet current 
seismic standards;
• A Buccaneer Beach Park Facilities Study to 
improve aging restrooms, picnic areas, and 
play areas;
• New Fire Station 1 and Emergency Operations 
Center;
• Funds for the Oceanside Fire Department 
training tower, drafting pit, and asphalt; and
• A Police Headquarters Expansion and Reloca-
tion 6tudy to facilitate officer relocation from 
the temporary facility and development of a 
permanent department with more compre-
hensive equipment.

7-19
DEVELOPER FEES
The City of Oceanside institutes developer or 
impact fees in order to provide funding for 
public facilities, schools, and parks. Residential 
developments pay $4.08 per square foot for 
OUSD and VUSD, $3.36 per square foot for BUSD 
and $3.48 per square foot for CUSD. Commercial 
Industrial developments pay $0.66 per square 
foot for OUSD and VUSD,  $0.54 per square foot 
for BUSD, and $0.56 per square foot for CUSD. 
Residential developments pay $2,621 per unit 
towards public facilities and approximately 
$4,431 per unit towards park funding as part 
of the Quimby Act. Commercial and industrial 
development pays $0.902 per square foot 
toward public facility development. 
Developer fees are also used to fund wastewa-
ter and water system capacity improvements. 
Developments are charged according to meter 
size, with multi-family and non-residential devel-
opments paying between $7,794 (5/8-inch meter) 
and $194,856 (4-inch meter) in wastewater 
capacity buy-in fees and between $5,680 (5/8-
inch meter) and $45,440 (2-inch meter) in water 
system capacity buy-in fees.
Oceanside is one of fourteen districts in the 
state to receive the designation of “California 
Cultural District” by the California Arts Council. 
California Cultural Districts highlight the thriv-
ing cultural diversity within local communities 
across California.  
The Oceanside California Cultural District (OCCD) 
is centered in Downtown Oceanside and is 3.6 
square miles.  The District includes a variety of 
cultural amenities, including the Star Theater 
at its northernmost point (Sportfisher Drive), 
Oceanside Museum of Art to the east (Nevada 
Street), Hill Street Country Club Gallery to the 
south (Minnesota Avenue) and Oceanside Pier to 
the west.  
STATE FUNDING
In 2019, the City received $8 million from the 
State to help implement a beach renovation proj-
ect. Construction began in 2020 and includes, 
upgrading the beach stairways, new public bath-
rooms, a police substation, maintenance and 
storage facilities for City operations, and a new 
public plaza with landscaping in the area just 
south of the pier.


7-20
EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
7.9 Key Planning Issues 
and Implications
• D e c l i n i n g  e n r o l l m e n t  a n d  s c h o o l 
consolidation as an opportunity for the 
City to acquire land.  Reflective of larger 
demographic shifts as a whole, OUSD has 
seen declining enrollment rates and has 
responded by consolidating some school 
facilities. OUSD’s school consolidation and 
asset sales could represent an opportunity for 
the City to Oceanside to explore acquisition of 
sites for other publicly beneficial uses, such 
as affordable housing, parkland, or other 
community-serving facilities. 
• Expanding facilities for Fire and Police 
de- partments and relocating the City 
Operations Center. In the past ten years, 
both the Oceanside Police Department and 
Oceanside Fire Department assessments 
have indicated that expanded facilities or 
facility relocations are needed. Additionally, 
the current City Operations Center location 
makes it difficult for Public :orks to address 
emergencies on the west side of town in a 
timely manner. While more current needs 
assessments will be required to account for 
changed conditions and population growth 
estimates, the General Plan Update should 
coordinate with these departments to support 
identification of appropriate new sites that 
optimize response times. The El Corazon site, 
for example, has been proposed as a potential 
future location for the City Operations Center. 
• Lack of funding for arts programming. The 
City of Oceanside includes several beloved 
cultural institutions such as the Oceanside 
Museum of Art, the California Surf Museum, 
and the -unior 6eau Pier Amphitheater. 
However, funding for arts and cultural 
programming is less than peer cities. In 2017, 
the Oceanside Arts Commission determined 
that Oceanside’s per-capita arts funding 
(14 cents) lags significantly behind that of 
neighboring cities Carlsbad and San Diego 
(more than $10). 
• Sea level rise and coastal erosion 
threatening Oceanside ’s facilities.
Oceanside’s coastal amenities are threatened 
by a combination of declining beach sand 
and sea level rise due to climate change. 
Future sea-level rise is expected to create 
a permanent rise in ocean water levels that 
would shift the water’s edge landward. 
Between 19 and 28 recreational buildings 
could be impacted by sea level rise, resulting 
in disrupted access and water damage. Water 
and flood-related damages at this site may 
impact emergency response capacity and 
response time. Currently, 17 beach access 
points fall within coastal hazard zones. Under 
continued climate change and sea level rise, 19 
to 24 of these access points may within hazard 
zones by the end of this century, along with 38 
to 40 acres of beach. The issues of sea level 
rise and coastal erosion are important for the 
City to plan for and are addressed in the recent 
Climate Action Plan and Energy Climate Action 
Element of the General Plan, both adopted in 
2019.

8-2
EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
8.1 Water Infrastructure
EXISTING SYSTEM
Water supply to the City is primarily pro-
vided through purchasing of both treated and 
untreated imported water from the San Diego 
County Water Authority (SDCWA) through five 
(5) aqueduct connections. In Fiscal Year 2019, 
88% percent of the water supply was provided 
by the SDCWA. The remaining 11 percent of 
water supply was provided by the Mission Basin 
*roundwater Purification Facility and 1 from 
recycled water supplies. Annual water supplied 
in Fiscal Year 2019 was approximately 22,645 
acre feet (AF). The distribution system consists of 
28 pressure zones, 597 miles of pipelines varying 
in size from 4 inches to 42 inches, 8 groundwa-
ter wells, 12 storage reservoirs, 9 booster pump 
stations, 5 imported water connections, and 69 
pressure regulating stations. As shown in Table 
1-1, recycled water is growing in supply both 
through advanced treatment of wastewater with 
the primary use of groundwater basin replenish-
ment as well as recycled water irrigation use on 
golf courses, the City’s sports park as well as the 
Whelan Lake Bird Sanctuary.
The City completed the last water Master Plan 
in 2015. At that time, the estimated build-
out growth potential was based on the 2002 
General Plan land use, including distribution 
of land use types and both near-term and 
long-range development plans. San Diego 
Association of Governments (SANDAG) 2050 
regional growth forecasting was used for pro-
jecting the City’s future water demand. Some 
of the South Morro Hills area was projected to 
be comprised of new low-density residences; 
however, this was considered uncertain and 
the impacts of this conversion of land from 
agricultural use to low-density residential use 
on the water distribution system were thus not 
reflected in future water demand. 6ignificant 
amounts of redevelopment were planned for 
the Downtown area around City Hall and to the 
beachfront.
Since 2005, water usage in Oceanside has been 
reducing on a per capita basis (see Figure 8-1).

Figure 8-1: Trends in Population and Water Supply by Source, 2005-2020


WATER INFRASTRUCTURE
8-5
Actual water usage was 122 gallons per capita 
per day (gpcd) in 2020 compared to a 2020 
target usage of 137 GPCD. Annual water demand 
is expected to be relatively flat through 2045, 
with an ultimate annual water demand of 
24,377 acre-feet per year, (Source 2020 UWMP - 
Woodard & Curran).
As a result of the very low projected increase in 
water demand, there were no recommended 
capacity projects within the 2015 Water Master 
Plan specifically to address population growth. 
The only projects that addressed a capacity 
deficiency were related to addressing changes 
in regional standard fire flow requirements.  1o 
change in regional fire flow requirements (based 
on land use) are expected for the 2020 master 
plan update.  
Existing water storage reservoirs were installed 
between 1956 and 1995. Ongoing reservoir 
upgrades and new water quality monitoring 
projects were identified in 15 to support the 
long-term reliability of the aging infrastructure. 
Between 2015 and 2020, reservoir projects 
focused on structural rehabilitation needs.
As a primary gravity fed water distribution 
system, the City has nine (9) pressure booster 
stations, with these stations only utilized under 
emergency conditions. Projects were identified 
in 2015 to add backup generators to these sta-
tions for increased reliability.
FUTURE NEEDS
The City will be preparing the 2020 Water Master 
Plan to update and reprioritize recommend 
improvement projects. A distribution system 
water model will be used for evaluating perfor-
mance of the system. Performance criteria is 
expected to be identical to past water master 
plans.
Two areas of the distribution system have been 
recognized by the City as underperforming, 
including the Peacock Hills pressure zone served 
by the Crestview and Old Grove pressure reduc-
ing stations (PRSs) and Fire Mountain pressure 
zone, as shown in Figure 8-2. Achieving adequate 
fire flow in this area has historically been accept-
able, but near the allowable limit. While small 
changes in domestic water demand is not typi-
cally a trigger for needing to increase capacity, 
for this pressure zone, the current water pres-
sure performance and small increase in average 
annual water demand due to development may 
trigger the need for adding a new supply pipe-
line to the City water transmission pipeline.   This 
new connection would allow the pressure zone 
to be served at a higher pressure and improve 
customer satisfaction. Similar to Peacock Hills, 
in the Fire Mountain pressure zone, there is a 
potential trend to increase residential density 
by consolidating parcels.  The following figure 
shows the location of the Peacock Hills pressure 
zone and Fire Mountain pressure zone.  
For consideration of recommended changes 
in the City land use as part of the General Plan 
Update, any resultant changes in parcel land 
use will need to be assessed as part of the 
water system hydraulic modeling effort during 
the 2020 Water Master Plan update. The water 
model consolidates individual parcels into larger 
polygons for the assessment fire flows. No 
changes in land use-based fire flow criteria are 
projected as part of the 2020 Water Master Plan.
Related to Citywide future water demand, 
the City has invested in the construction of an 
advanced water treatment system at the San 
Luis Rey Water Reclamation Facility (SLRWRF) 
and is expanding the recycled water distribution 
network.  In 2018, the City expanded the tertiary 
treatment capacity of the SLRWRF to 3.0 MGD 
and allowed for future expansion of up to 10.5 
MGD. This system provides a direct supply of 
recycled water to large irrigation and commercial 
demands, supplementing current potable water 
demand. At the end of 2021, the system will 
also include the injection of advanced treated 
water into the groundwater network to further 
support the long-term sustainability of the 
groundwater basin as a source of potable water. 
The combined effort will both reduce overall 
demand of potable water and reduce reliance 
on imported potable water from the SDCWA by 
approximately 30 percent.

WATER INFRASTRUCTURE
8-7
8.2 Sewer System
EXISTING SYSTEM
The existing sewer collection system consists of 
486 miles of gravity pipeline, 50 miles of force 
mains, 36 lift stations and two wastewater 
treatment plants. There are also 201 miles of 
private sewer laterals that connect customers to 
the sewer system. Wastewater is treated either 
at the La Salina Wastewater Treatment Plant 
(WWTP) or the San Luis Rey WWTP. Both treat-
ment plants discharge treated wastewater to the 
Pacific 2cean through ocean outfalls. The system 
collects and treats an average of 12.5 million gal-
lons per day (MGD) during dry weather, and up 
to 30.7 MGD during wet weather. Wet weather 
peaking factors are commonly around 2 to 2.5 
times average dry weather flows.
The 2015 Sewer Master Plan (SMP) identifies 
near term (2015-2020) and long term (2021-
2050) capital improvements program (CIP) 
projects designed to increase capacity, replace 
or rehabilitate deteriorating assets in order to 
provide reliable services, prevent environmen-
tal contamination, and expand the wastewater 
collection system to adequately meet current 
demands and future demands based on the 
SANDAG 2050 Regional Growth Forecast. While 
there are no overall treatment capacity issues 
currently, CIP prioritization is based on urgency 
to mitigate existing deficiencies and condition 
issues and provide capacity for future growth 
including near term development projects (e.g. El 
Corazon, Terraza RDO Village XII, Ocean Pointe, 
and The Villages of Morro Hills). Pipeline rehabil-
itation accounts for nearly 62% of the total CIP 
costs through 2050. The largest capital project 
consists of the combined new La Salina Pump 
Station, associated forcemain and improve-
ments to San Luis Rey WRF. Rehabilitation proj-
ects were identified through an age-based asset 
management model that projects useful life for 
various materials, systems and technologies. 
6ince 15, the City has made extensive efforts 
to inspect the condition of the collection system 
as well as implement a significant amount of 
projects to reduce I&I, to ensure spills do not 
enter waterways and to repair/rehabilitate sev-
eral of the larger sewer force mains.
FUTURE NEEDS
During the capacity analysis portion of the 
master planning effort, only large sewer inter-
ceptor pipelines were included in the mod-
eling process. These pipelines accounted for 
approximately 30 percent of the entire collec-
tion system. For wet weather capacity analy-
sis, pipelines were allowed to flow up to 100 
percent of capacity before being triggered as 
undersized. The evaluation criteria for the 2020 
Sewer Master Plan will stipulate the maximum 
allowable wet weather flow capacity to a depth 
to diameter ratio of 0.85. Modeled flows that 
exceed this criterion will be considered under-
sized and candidates for upsizing.
The study area for the SMP closely coincides 
with the City boundaries with the exception 
of the South Morro Hills area in the northeast 
section of the City, as shown in Figure 8-3. 
The South Morro Hills area does not have any 
sewer infrastructure installed at the time of 
the SMP evaluation and all sewage in the area 
is disposed of via septic systems. The South 
Morro Hills Community Plan being prepared by 
the City is looking at potential for agritourism 
and increased residential development in the 
area. Changes in the South Morro Hills area to 
residential land use will require extension of the 
collection system to service new development, 
and as a result the existing downstream collec-
tion system will have increased flows and may 
require upsizing to operate below the maximum 
allowable wet weather flow capacity depth to 
diameter ratio of 0.85.

WATER INFRASTRUCTURE
8.3 Stormwater System 
EXISTING SYSTEM
The existing stormwater system consists of 
2,790 gravity flow drainage facilities defined 
as individual portions of the drainage system 
separated by cleanouts, manholes, or changes 
in size, slope, or material for a combined total 
length of 477,450 linear feet. In addition, each 
facility is classified by the watershed in which 
the facility collects and discharges stormwater 
runoff. The seven distinct watersheds within the 
City used for classifying stormwater system facil-
ities include Pilgrim Creek, San Luis Rey River, 
Garrison Creek, Loma Alta Creek, Buena Vista 
Creek, Aqua Hedionda Head :aters, and Pacific 
Ocean/Beach Area, as shown in Figure 8-3. 
The City first completed a Master Plan of 
Drainage (MPD) in 2005, and then performed 
its most recent update in 2013. The 2013 update 
was designed to identify changes in the storm-
water system as a result of various develop-
ments since 2005, create a new computerized 
model that is up-to-date with the current system 
configuration and available technology, and per-
form hydraulic analyses to determine which, if 
any, of the drainage facilities are inadequate to 
convey flows for the 1-, 5- and 1-year storm 
events. Inadequate facilities were defined where 
the hydraulic analysis indicated that the hydrau-
lic grade line for a particular sized storm event 
would exceed the surface elevation (ground, 
street, manhole rim) at a given point. Table 8-1 
summarized the results of inadequate facilities 
by watershed:

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
FUTURE SYSTEM
The current National Pollution Discharge 
Elimination Systems (NPDES) Regional Municipal 
Separate Storm Sewer System (MS4) Permit (MS4 
Permit), under which the City is a co-permittee, 
requires new projects to install and manage 
best management practices (BMPs) onsite that 
mitigate project effects to the City’s existing 
MS4. New projects must determine the poten-
tial changes in onsite runoff due to increases in 
impervious surfaces and/or drainage area alter-
ations, then install and manage BMPs that regu-
late offsite discharges to mimic pre-project con-
ditions. This regulation is intended to maintain 
(or reduce) pre-project stormwater peak flows 
in order to reduce potential for erosion, provide 
an opportunity to enhance water quality, and to 
prevent an overflow of the stormwater system.
The current 06 permit effectively requires any 
future development upon a parcel to ensure that 
the extent of new development does not result 
in an increase in peak stormwater runoff.  This is 
particularly relevant for new development that 
results in the construction of new impervious 
surfaces.  As a result, if the existing stormwa-
ter collection system has adequate capacity 
to convey current peak stormwater flows, the 
MS4 permit protects the stormwater collection 
system from future growth and development 
within the drainage basin.  
As stated above, and within the 2013 Master Plan 
of Drainage, there are improvements needed 
within the existing stormwater infrastructure to 
meet current peak drainage flows.  Due to the 
application of the MS4 Permit, recommended 
changes in future development patterns of the 
City identified within the 2020 General Plan 
should not result in changes to the peak storm-
water flows conveyed within the stormwater 
collection system.


PARKS AND RECREATIONAL OPEN SPACE
8-13
8.4 Key Planning Issues 
and Implications
• Water infrastructure planning and po- 
tential residential development in South 
Morro Hills. When the 2015 Water Master Plan 
was developed, the Morro Hills Development 
was projected to be comprised of new low-
density residences. While discussed in the 
2015 Master Plan, the Morro Hills Development 
was considered uncertain and therefore the 
impacts of this conversion upon the water 
distribution system were not considered as 
part of the master planning effort. Water 
conveyance updates and capacity assessments 
in the 2020 Water Master Plan will need to 
account for land use decisions made as part of 
the South Morro Hills Community Plan Update. 
• Water supply pipeline needs. The Peacock 
Hills pressure zone served by the Crestview 
and Old Grove pressure reducing stations 
(PRSs) and Fire Mountain pressure zone 
have been recognized by the Cit y as 
underperforming. Two areas of the distribution 
system have been recognized by the City as 
underperforming, including the (sentence just 
ends here - please complete sentence). This 
new connection would allow the pressure zone 
to be served at a higher pressure and improve 
customer satisfaction. 
• City water treatment upgrades and over-
all water demand. The advanced water 
treatment system at the San Luis Rey Water 
Reclamation Facility and completion of the 
expansion of the recycled water distribution 
network will provide a direct supply of recycled 
water to large irrigation and commercial 
demands, supplementing current potable 
water demand. The system will also include the 
injection of advanced treated water into the 
groundwater network to further support the 
long-term sustainability of the groundwater 
basin. The combined effort will reduce overall 
demand and reduce reliance on imported 
potable water.
• Sewer infrastructure extension in South 
Morro Hills. All sewage in South Morro Hills 
area is disposed of via septic systems and thus 
was not included in 2015 Sewer Master Plan 
analysis. Changes in the Morro Hills area to 
residential land use, which could include the 
addition of 600 to 1,000 homes, will require 
extension of the collection system to service 
new development, and as a result the existing 
downstream collection system will have 
increased flows and may require upsi]ing.
• Stormwater CIP projects and minimum 
storm event conveyance. Stormwater facility 
inadequacies are not based on population 
growth; rather, they are based on conditions 
prior to establishment of BMP requirements 
and/or advancements in hydrologic modeling. 
Because population growth should not be a 
factor on stormwater system capacity, the 
prioritization of stormwater CIP projects 
should address facilities based on the 
minimum storm event conveyance criteria 
and consider the location of these projects 
in proximity to other, non-stormwater CIP 
projects. Any major public infrastructure 
upgrades that may require excavation within 
or along roadways and/or changes in drainage 
patterns should also evaluate whether or not a 
stormwater project identified in the 13 0P' 
is within the proposed construction footprint, 
and if so, how or if the stormwater project can 
be addressed in a comprehensive design to 
create efficiencies. 


ENVIRONMENTAL JUSTICE AND PUBLIC HEALTH

ENVIRONMENTAL JUSTICE 
AND PUBLIC HEALTH


9-2
EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
9.1 Environmental 
Justice
ENVIRONMENTAL JUSTICE AND EQUITY
The City of Oceanside’s current General Plan 
describes the City as a place where everyone 
can live a long, healthy, and opportunity-filled 
life. However, not all people have the opportu-
nity to live their healthiest lives because of envi-
ronmental inequities, which means that certain 
geographies and groups of people unfairly and 
disproportionately experience higher exposure 
to pollution, greater negative health impacts, 
and less access to health-supporting features, 
such as green space and parks, safe and afford-
able places to live, quality jobs and schools, and 
stores that meet basic needs. 
Environmental justice is a movement to take 
concerted action to remedy environmental con-
ditions that result in economic and health dispar-
ities. Ultimately, environmental justice and social 
equity mean the fair treatment and meaningful 
involvement of all groups so that everyone has 
the chance to live in a healthy environment. 
DISADVANTAGED COMMUNITIES
Under California law (SB 1000), cities and other 
local jurisdictions must address environmental 
justice by developing policies for issues that 
affect disadvantaged communities, or commu-
nities that experience high levels of pollution, 
socioeconomic stress, and health impacts. SB 
1000 identifies the California Communities 
Environmental Health Screening Tool 3.0 
(CalEnviroScreen) as the primary method 
for identifying disadvantaged communities. 
Developed by the California Environmental 
Protection Agency (CalEPA), CalEnviroScreen 
uses environmental, health, and socioeconomic 
information to produce scores for every census 
tract in the state, thereby identifying commu-
nities that are most vulnerable to pollution’s 
effects. The score assigned to each census tract 
is a product of exposure to pollution, adverse 
environmental effects caused by pollution, pres-
ence of sensitive populations (i.e., those with 
asthma or cardiovascular disease), and socioeco-
nomic factors that increase vulnerability (i.e., low 
household income, low educational attainment, 
and unemployment). A disadvantaged commu-
nity is defined as one which scores in the top 
25th percentile of CalEnviroScreen’s cumulative 
score. 


ENVIRONMENTAL JUSTICE AND PUBLIC HEALTH
9-3
Overall CalEnviroScreen scores are calculated 
from scores of two groups of indicators, pol-
lution burden, and population characteristics 
scores. Pollution burden represents the poten-
tial exposures to pollutants, such as traffic, 
drinking water contaminants, toxic releases, and 
other sources, and the adverse environmental 
conditions caused by pollution, such as ground-
water threats, cleanup sites, solid waste sites, 
or impaired water bodies. Population character-
istics represent biological traits, health status, 
or community characteristics that can result 
in increased vulnerability to pollution, such as 
health conditions or socioeconomic factors like 
poverty or unemployment. 
Using these criteria, there are no census tracts 
within the Planning Area designated as disad-
vantaged communities. However, some tracts 
bear a disproportionate environmental burden 
compared to other census tracts in the Planning 
Area.  Some tracts in the Planning Area have 
individual pollution burden or population char-
acteristics scores above 70 . In general, tracts 
with higher burdens are located in the west of 
the city, due in part to higher relative pollution 
burdens along the I-5 corridor. Census tracts 
with cumulative CalEnviroScreen scores over 65 
percent, including Census Tracts 82 and 85.09, 
located in the south of Townsite and Loma Alta 
neighborhoods, are shown in Table 9-1 and in 
Figure 9-1.

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
9.2 Public Health   
One of the goals of the environmental justice 
movement is to improve conditions that affect 
people’s health. Determinants of health include 
social factors and physical conditions of the 
place in which people are born, live, learn, work, 
play, and age. Social determinants include pov-
erty, employment, educational attainment, and 
housing, while physical determinants include 
environmental quality, public transit accessibil-
ity, and walking distance to services. Both social 
and physical factors influence health.
HEALTH OUTCOMES
Evidence increasingly shows how interactions 
between the social and physical environments 
contribute significantly to an array of health 
risks and outcomes. While San Diego County has 
consistently ranked among the top 15 healthiest 
counties in California since 2016, according to 
California Health Interview Survey data, the City 
of Oceanside has higher rates than the county 
for adults ever diagnosed with diabetes (9.1 
percent versus 8.2 percent), low-income adults 
experiencing food insecurity (8.3 versus 6.1 per-
cent), adult obesity (26.1 versus 25 percent), and 
elevated weight and obesity for ages 12-17 (46.4 
versus 30.4 percent).1     
At the census tract level, health disparities 
become even more stark, with several census 
tracts in the Planning Area facing worse health 
outcomes than others. Figures 9-2 through 9-4 
show how census tracts within the Planning 
Area rank among all California counties for rates 
of asthma, high blood pressure, and low birth 
weight.

Edition. 2018

rtega

9-6
Figure 9-2 shows how rates of asthma within 
the Planning Area compare to the rest of tracts 
in California. Census tracts in and around 
downtown Oceanside--as well as tract 194.04, 
located along Oceanside Boulevard in southeast 
Oceanside--display elevated rates of asthma 
compared to the rest of the state; these neigh-
borhoods have asthma rates higher than 50 to 
73 percent of California census tracts. These 
census tracts also overlap with areas of the City 
that have higher rates of poverty and cardio-
vascular disease, as shown in Figures 9-3 and 
9-5. The causes of asthma are unknown, but 
both genetic and environmental factors can be 
involved, including poor air quality. Air quality in 
the downtown and Oceanside Boulevard areas 
may be affected by vehicular traffic along major 
corridors, which is likely to reduce over time 
as emissions standards become increasingly 
stringent. Pollution in the area has also likely 
decreased in recent years due to the decommis-
sioning and planned demolition of the nearby 
Encina Power Station, located just south of the 
area in Carlsbad.  Additionally, by facilitating 
alternative modes of transportation, the City can 
reduce vehicle miles traveled (VMT) and associ-
ated emissions. 
As seen In Figure 9-3, patterns of cardiovascular 
disease within Oceanside are similar to those 
for asthma, with ddowntown Oceanside expe-
riencing rates of cardiovascular disease that are 
higher than 50 to 74 percent of census tracts 
in California  . There are many risk factors for 
developing cardiovascular disease including age, 
diet, lack of exercise, smoking and exposure to 
air pollution. Exposure to outdoor air pollution 
following a heart attack has been shown to 
increase the risk of death. In addition to people 
with a past heart attack, the effects of air pol-
lution may also be greater in the elderly and 
people with other preexisting health conditions.
Figure 9-4 shows that two census tracts - Tract 
185.17, located along Vista Way in south central 
Oceanside, and Tract 186.11, corresponding 
closely to the boundaries of the South Morro 
Hills area - have incidences of low birth weight 
that place them in the top quartile for the state, 
meaning that low birth weight rates in these 
tracts rank higher than 75 percent of all census 
tracts in California. Poor nutrition, lack of prena-
tal care, stress, and smoking by the mother are 
known to increase the risk of having a low-birth-
weight baby. Studies suggest that pollution could 
also be a factor.2  
2  Rogers, J. F., &amp; Dunlop, A. L. (2006, July 1). Air Pollution 
and Very Low Birth Weight Infants: A Target Population? 
American Academy of Pediatrics. https://pediatrics.
aappublications.org/content/118/1/156.

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
SOCIAL DETERMINANTS OF PUBLIC 
HEALTH
Poverty, Unemployment, and 
Educational Attainment
Social determinants can increase stress levels 
and/or make healthy living difficult, which can in 
turn cause populations to be more sensitive to 
the effects of pollution. For example, residents 
of high-poverty neighborhoods may be more 
likely to be exposed to toxic materials in homes, 
dangerous streets, and pervasive advertising 
promoting harmful substances than residents 
of higher-income neighborhoods.3 High-poverty 
neighborhoods also may have limited options 
for healthy food and safe outdoor physical activ-
ity, and fewer opportunities for education and 
high-quality employment than higher-income 
neighborhoods.4  These circumstances can 
result in a multi-generational cycle of poor nutri-
tion, poor health, and limited social, educational, 
and economic opportunities.
In 2018, approximately 29.4 percent of the City 
of Oceanside’s population was living 200 percent 
below the federal poverty level, on par with the 
county’s current rate of 29.3 percent. Though 
CalEnviroScreen uses now outdated ACS data 
from 2011-2015 for its 2018 assessments, two of 
Oceanside’s downtown census tracts, 182 and 
185.09, had poverty rates of 58 and 57 percent, 
respectively. These tracts, shown in Figure 9-5, 
ranked in the top quartile for poverty rates, or 
higher than 75 percent of the census tracts in 
California.
3  The Robert Wood Johnson Foundation, Center on Social 
Disparities in Health, and the Build Healthy Places network, 
Making the Case for Linking Community Development and 
Health, 2015. 
4  The Robert Wood Johnson Foundation, Center on Social 
Disparities in Health, and the Build Healthy Places network, 
Making the Case for Linking Community Development and 
Health, 2015. 
Stress from long-term unemployment can lead 
to chronic illnesses, such as heart disease, which 
can shorten a person’s life.5 In 2018, the unem-
ployment rate in the City of Oceanside was 6.2 
percent, approximately equivalent to the county 
average of 6.4 percent. As seen In Figure 9-6, 
Tracts 185.10 and 185.16, both located near cen-
tral Oceanside, had unemployment rates in the 
top quartile of CalEnviroScreen-ranked tracts, 
meaning their unemployment rates were higher 
than 75 percent of the census tracts in California.
Low educational attainment also impacts 
health. Educational attainment correlates most 
strongly with life expectancy and most health 
status measures.6 Additionally, several studies 
have associated educational attainment with 
susceptibility to the health impacts of envi-
ronmental pollutants.7 According to 2018 ACS 
data, within the Planning Area, approximately 
13.3 percent of Oceanside residents have no 
high school diploma, higher than the county 
average of 12.9 percent. In Figure 9-7, Based on 
CalEnviroScreen rankings, several of these tracts 
had levels of educational attainment that are 
among the lowest 25 percent in California. These 
tracts are scattered across the City. While edu-
cational attainment is correlated with multiple 
factors, research indicates parental education 
and income is strongly associated with children’s 
educational achievement.8
5  Brand J. E. The Far-Reaching Impact of Job Loss and 
Unemployment. Annual review of sociology, 41, 359-375, 2015. 
6  Winkleby, M. A., Jatulis, D. E., Frank, E., & Fortmann, S. P. 
Socioeconomic status and health: how education, income, and 
occupation contribute to risk factors for cardiovascular disease. 
American journal of public health, 82(6), 816-20, 1992.
7  The Robert Wood Johnson Foundation, Issue Brief: Why does 
education matter so much to health? 2013.
8  https://phys.org/news/2014-02-parental-income-strong-
predictor-student.html

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
Housing
Health and Safety
Access to safe and adequate affordable housing 
is a critical component of environmental jus-
tice. According to American Community Survey 
Data, 1% of homeowners and 6% of renters in 
Oceanside face two or more physical or finan-
cial issues. These issues can include a lack of 
complete plumbing facilities, a lack of complete 
kitchen facilities, more than one occupant per 
room, and housing costs as a percentage of 
household income greater than 30 percent. The 
data is used to identify those homes in which the 
quality of living and housing can be considered 
substandard.9  
Studies have indicated that renters have higher 
rates of material hardship, which might force 
acceptance of inadequate housing conditions.10,11 
Figure 9-8 shows the percentage of renters 
facing two or more issues by census tract across 
the City. In the northern portion of the Guajome 
neighborhood and the majority of the Lake 
neighborhood, 20-30 percent of renters face 
two or more issues, the highest percent in the 
City and much higher than the citywide average. 
Portions of East Side Capistrano, Airport, and 
east Guajome as well as Morro Hills and Loma 
Alta also face higher than average rates of sub-
standard housing. 
9  https://www.socialexplorer.com/data/ACS2017_5yr/
metadata/?ds=ACS17_5yr&table=B25123; https://
censusreporter.org/topics/housing/
10  California Healthy Places Index. Housing Habitability. 
https://healthyplacesindex.org/policy-actions/housing-
habitability/ Accessed May 2021.
11  Scally, C. and Gonzalez D. “Renters ar Renters are more 
likely than homeowners to struggle with paying for basic 
needs.” Urban Institute, 2018. https://www.urban.org/
urban-wire/renters-are-more-likely-homeowners-struggle-
paying-basic-needs#:~:text=Renters%20face%20greater%20
financial%20uncertainty,to%20cover%20an%20emergency%20
expense. Accessed May 2021.

Aﬀordability
Generally, a household is considered “rent-bur-
dened” if 30 percent or more of the gross house-
hold income is spent on rent, which reduces 
the availability of funds for schooling, childcare, 
groceries, transportation, and other household 
costs.12 According to 2018 ACS data, approxi-
mately 57.3 percent of renting households in 
Oceanside pay more than 30 percent of their 
household income towards rent, as shown in 
Figure 9-9. 
While the majority of the Oceanside’s residents 
are homeowners (56 percent), many of these 
homeowners pay a significant portion of their 
income towards housing costs. Median home 
values have increased by about 7 percent over 
the past 17 years, from $429,700 in 2010 to 
$460,100 in 2018.13 Thirty four percent of house-
holds with a mortgage pay more than 30 percent 
of their household income towards housing 
costs, which includes all mortgage principal 
payments, interest payments, real estate taxes, 
property insurance, homeowner fees, condo or 
coop fees and utilities (not including telephone 
or cable television).14
12  Ibid.
13  American Community Survey, 2010 and 2018.
14  American Community Survey, 2014-2018. 
Community Engagement 
Residents’ ability to engage in community life 
contributes to feelings of social belonging and 
good emotional and physical health. Community 
engagement programs and activities also pro-
motes environmental justice by providing resi-
dents with regular opportunities to share their 
environmental and health concerns and make 
their voice heard in the policymaking process. 
Two barriers that can inhibit sustained and 
meaningful community participation include 
linguistic isolation and lack of internet access.
Linguistic Isolation
Many households where little or no English is 
spoken experience linguistic isolation. Adults 
who are unable to communicate comfortably 
and effectively in English often have trouble 
talking to medical professionals and providers 
of social services, and consequently may not 
get the medical care or social support they and 
their children need. Additionally, members of lin-
guistically isolated households may not hear or 
understand important information when there is 
an emergency or an important community issue 
is under consideration.

Figure 9-10: Linguistic Isolation


9-18
EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
Approximately five percent of Oceanside’s 
households are linguistically isolated, in that 
household members lack fluency in English. 
Of these linguistically isolated households, 67 
percent speak Spanish. The census tracts with 
the highest proportion of linguistically isolated 
households include Tracts 186.10, 185.19, 185.09, 
and 182. These tracts are shown in Figure 9-10.
Efforts to engage the City’s Spanish-speaking 
community in the General Plan Update process 
have included Spanish language announce-
ments of workshops and other outreach events, 
translation services at outreach events, and a 
Spanish language survey (available both online 
and in hard-copy).  The project team has posted 
Spanish language announcements online and 
at community resource and recreation centers, 
community health centers, churches, and local 
businesses that regularly serve the Spanish-
speaking community.  Despite these efforts, 
input from Spanish-speaking community mem-
bers has thus far been limited.  As the General 
Plan Update unfolds, the project team will 
continue to explore ways to better engage the 
Spanish-speaking community.  
Internet Access
Internet access facilitates access to online com-
munity organizing, job and educational opportu-
nities, and a variety of other vital personal and 
community resources and activities. The COVID-
19 pandemic has illustrated the significant role 
that the internet plays in facilitating communi-
cation and community building and ensuring 
equity of opportunity with regard to education, 
employment, and access to important public 
health information. While the Planning Area has 
rates of internet access on par with the county, 
access rates vary across Oceanside census 
tracts. According to 2018 ACS 5-year estimates, 
areas with limited internet connectivity include 
downtown Oceanside, tracts along the north and 
south of Mission Avenue, and tract 193.02 near 
Oceanside and College boulevards. These tracts 
are shown in Figure 9-11.     Barriers to internet 
access can include lack of access to infrastruc-
ture, but most often are related to the financial 
burden of subscribing to an internet service and 
purchasing the necessary hardware.

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
PHYSICAL DETERMINANTS OF PUBLIC 
HEALTH
Pollution Burdens in Oceanside
Environmental pollution can take a variety of 
forms, including pollution of the water, air, and 
soil. All forms can have detrimental impacts on 
human health. The City of Oceanside enjoys 
relatively good air quality, benefiting from an 
oceanfront setting and a relative lack of legacy 
heavy industrial, manufacturing, and refinery 
uses. However, there are a few environmental 
concerns within the City, including pesticides, 
automobile emissions, and water quality. 
Exhaust from cars and trucks is the major source 
of air pollution in much of California. Exhaust 
fumes contain toxic chemicals that can damage 
DNA, cause cancer, make breathing difficult, 
and cause low birth weight and premature 
births. Children who live or go to schools near 
busy roads have higher rates of asthma and 
other lung diseases than children in areas fur-
ther from roads. Major roads can also generate 
noise in nearby neighborhoods. According to 
CalEnviroScreen, tracts ranking in the highest 
quartile in the concentration of listed contam-
inants (?) are those nearest to Oceanside’s 
western border and the major transportation 
corridors of I-5 and Highway 78. These tracts are 
shown in Figure 9-11.
Most drinking water in California meets require-
ments for health and safety. However, water 
can become contaminated with chemicals or 
bacteria. Both natural sources and human activ-
ities can contaminate drinking water, and both 
sources can introduce bacteria, viruses and para-
sites. Natural sources can include rocks, soil, and 
fire.   Human sources include industrial opera-
tions, water conveyance systems, automobiles, 
sewage, and runoff from farms and urban areas 
. Tracts in the top quartile are shown in Figure 
9-12. Drinking water in these census tracts  have 
especially high levels of 1,2,3-TrichloroProprane 
(TCP), a solvent, cleaning and degreasing agent, 
and paint and varnish remover which can cause 
eye and throat Irritation, affect muscle coordina-
tion and concentration, harm kidney function, 
and cause cancer. 
Pesticides are chemicals used to control insects, 
weeds, and plant disease. Over 1,000 pesticides 
are registered for use in California. They can 
be applied to fields by air, farm machinery, or 
workers on the ground. Exposure to pesticides 
can cause short term health impacts such as 
eye and respiratory irritation, as well as long 
term impacts such as birth defects and cancer. 
Farmworker families and those who live near 
fields are the most likely to be exposed to high 
levels of pesticides. Based on CalEnviroScreen 
data, a number of census tracts in Oceanside are 
subject to some of the highest levels of pesticide 
exposure among CalEnviroScreen-ranked tracts 
in California, with some tracts in the top quartile 
(ranging from the 79th to 99th percentile). As 
shown in Figure 9-13, most of these tracts are 
within or in close proximity to South Morro Hills, 
Oceanside’s agricultural district.

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
Walkability and Access to Transit
The City of Oceanside has a Walk Score of 36 
(on a scale of 1 to 100), meaning that it is some-
what walkable but that most errands require a 
car.15 Amenities such as parks, restaurants, and 
grocery stores are within walking distance of 
many residents, but not in all areas of the City. 
As seen in the Figure 9-15, commercial ameni-
ties are grouped in downtown Oceanside, along 
segments of SR 76, and in the Tri-City area. 
While commercial amenities along segments of 
Oceanside Boulevard are in close proximity to 
residential areas, steep topography and other 
physical barriers make it challenging for resi-
dents to walk or bicycle to these venues.  
Convenient access to public transit also helps 
community members increase physical activity 
and decrease vehicle miles traveled. Almost one-
third of Americans who commute to work via 
public transit meet their daily requirements for 
physical activity (30 or more minutes a day) by 
walking as a part of their daily life, including to 
and from transit stops.16 While walkability and 
access to transit stations is one part of transit 
access, it is also important that people can access 
their destinations quickly and conveniently. 
15  Walk Score measures the walkability of a location by 
analyzing walking routes to nearby amenities. Points are 
awarded based on the distance to amenities in each category. 
Walk Score also measures pedestrian friendli-ness by analyzing 
population density and road metrics such as block length and 
intersection density. 
16  L. Besser and A. Dannenberg, “Walking to Public Transit: Steps 
to Help Meet Physical Activity Recommenda-tions”. Vol. 32, Issue 
4, American Journal of Preventive Medicine, at 273-280, 2005.
Transfers, extra stops, and distance from desti-
nation can all contribute to inconvenience along 
a transit route. The City should prioritize more 
direct routes between disadvantaged and tran-
sit-dependent communities and colleges, medi-
cal centers and employment centers. 
As seen in Table 9-2, very few residents within 
the Planning Area currently utilize public transit 
or engage in active transportation. Almost 88 
percent of residents in the Planning Area com-
mute to work via car, truck, or van. Of those 
who drive to work, 90 percent drive alone, while 
10 percent carpool. Less than two percent of 
residents use active transportation (walking or 
biking) to get to work, and only three percent use 
public transportation. At the county level, 3.6 
percent of the population uses active transporta-
tion and three percent use public transit to com-
mute to work. Compared to San Diego County, 
the City of Oceanside has slightly lower rates of 
walking for recreation and fitness (?) (39.5 versus 
41.2 percent).  
Dispersed land use patterns and the separation 
of residential and non-residential land uses 
contribute significantly to the lack of walkability 
in Oceanside, particularly outside of the City’s 
major commercial corridors.  Some of the City’s 
most isolated neighborhoods include South 
Morro Hills, Ocean Hills, Peacock, Guajome, and 
North Valley, all of which lie in eastern portions 
of the City and feature mostly single-family 
housing.

Access to Parks, Recreation, Healthy 
Foods, and Health Services
Safe and convenient access to parks and recre-
ation is an important community health indi-
cator. Regular physical activity has many phys-
ical health benefits, including a reduced risk of 
coronary heart disease, obesity, hypertension, 
stroke, some cancers, and other conditions that 
contribute to premature mortality.17 Regular 
physical activity also has many attendant emo-
tional health benefits, such as reduced depres-
sion, anxiety, stress, as well as improved mood 
and the ability to perform tasks and effectively 
navigate challenging life events.18 The National 
Parks Service and the Centers for Disease 
Control and Prevention use a half-mile radius 
to evaluate access to parks and other commu-
nity amenities, while the 2019 Oceanside Parks 
and Recreation Master Plan evaluates access 
at a one-quarter mile radius.19 According to 
the Parks and Recreation Master Plan, approx-
imately 56 percent of Oceanside residents live 
within one-quarter mile   of a park or community 
space, compared to 62 percent of residents of 
San Diego County. Moreover, as noted earlier, 
topography and other physical barriers (e.g., 
waterways, railroads) often complicate access to 
such amenities, even for those who live nearby. 
For more information on park access, please see 
Chapter 6: Parks and Open Space in this ECR.
In addition to being walkable and well-served by 
public transit, a healthy community also has con-
venient access to medical services and healthy 
foods. When health care facilities are accessible 
via public transit, medical care is more readily 
accessible to those who do not drive or own 
cars. There are multiple medical facilities located 
within the Planning Area, ranging from large hos-
pitals and medical complexes operated at the
17  U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, Physical 
Activity and Health: A Report of the Surgeon Gen-eral, Atlanta: 
U.S. Department of Health and Human Services, Centers for 
Disease Control and Prevention, Na-tional Center for Chronic 
Disease Prevention and Health Promotion, 4–8, 1996.
18  Ibid.
19  Merriam, D.; Bality, A.; Stein, J.; Boehmer, T. Improving 
Public Health through Public Parks and Trails: Eight Common 
Measures. Summary report. US Department of Health and 
Human Services, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and 
US Department of the Interior, National Park Service, 5, 2017.
Tri-City Medical Center and the Naval Hospital at 
Camp Pendleton, mid-size clinics run by Scripps 
Health and Kaiser Permanente, and  smaller 
health centers run by TrueCare, the County of 
San Diego, and Vista Community Clinic, which 
operates four Federally Qualified Health Centers 
(FQHC) in Oceanside at 4002 Vista Way, 517 N 
Horne Street, 818 Pier View Way, and 4700 North 
River Road.  
Access to healthy foods is also important to a 
community’s health. The presence of a super-
market in a neighborhood is linked to higher 
fruit and vegetable consumption, as well as a 
reduced incidence of obesity.20 As shown in 
Figure 9-16, the City of Oceanside contains sev-
eral large chain supermarkets, with most clus-
tered in commercial shopping centers. Large 
chains (over 6,000 square feet) and smaller 
grocery stores are mainly located in downtown 
Oceanside, the major commercial corridors of 
Vista Way and Mission Avenue, and to a lesser 
extent, Oceanside Boulevard. The majority of 
Oceanside’s convenience stores are similarly 
distributed, falling predominately either in 
downtown Oceanside or along major corridors. 
Outside of these areas, there is a lack of conve-
nient healthy food options within walkable dis-
tance, especially for residential neighborhoods 
between the corridors. 
20  Inagami, S., et al., “You Are Where You Shop: Grocery 
Store Locations, Weight, and Neighborhoods”, Vol. 31, Issue 1, 
American Journal of Preventative Medicine, 10-17. See also K. 
Morland et al., “Supermarkets, Other Food Stores, and Obesity: 
The Atherosclerosis Risk in Communities Study”, Vol. 30, Issue 4, 
American Journal of Preventative Medicine, 333-339, 2006.

EXISTING CONDITIONS REPORT
9-28
9.3 Key Planning Issues 
and Objectives
• 
Creating environments that support 
better health outcomes.  While San Diego 
County is one of the healthiest counties in 
California, Oceanside residents experience 
higher rates of diabetes, food insecurity, 
and adult and child obesity. Within the 
City, populations that live in certain census 
tracts, especially those close to downtown 
Oceanside, have rates of asthma and high 
blood pressure that are higher than 60 per-
cent of census tracts in California. While 
health outcomes are the result of a number 
of factors, including genetics and behav-
ior, the General Plan Update will shape the 
environmental conditions that may impact 
health.
• 
Addressing communities with higher 
socioeconomic vulnerability. Some com-
munities in Oceanside, including tracts in 
central Oceanside, have poverty and unem-
ployment rates that rank in the highest quar-
tile in all of California. Others have some of 
the lowest rates of educational attainment 
in California. These factors make it difficult 
to live a healthy and opportunity-filled life. 
In wake of COVID-19, economic development 
programs, job training, and public services 
will be essential in supporting families and 
individuals with less resources, particularly 
residents in these geographic areas.
• 
Conducting targeted outreach and pro-
viding additional options for community 
engagement with linguistically, socially, 
or technologically isolated groups. Areas 
where people are not civically engaged and 
more linguistically isolated are also typically 
those areas where there is a lack of internet 
access   and worse health outcomes.  The 
City should explore other means of out-
reach to these communities in policymaking, 
information distribution, and engagement, 
including by mail, through existing com-
munity groups and events, and in multiple 
languages.  The City can also consider ways 
to reduce the “digital divide” faced by many 
lower-income and cost-burdened house-
holds such as implementing community 
broadband, providing financial assistance for 
data and hardware, and improving housing 
affordability. For example, a current goal of 
the Library department is to provide inno-
vative ways for customers to obtain library 
services and materials. This can Include a 
variety of technology solutions for informa-
tion delivery, and efforts to find out what 
tools community members have to take 
advantage of, and how to address the lack 
of such tools, as well as outreach to make 
the community aware of what the Library 
can provide. Outreach plans include devel-
opment of a translation team to assist with 
bridging linguistic gaps.
• 
Addressing Oceanside renters’ high 
housing cost burden. Nearly 60 percent of 
Oceanside renters are rent cost burdened. 
Median home prices have increased since 
2010, and nearly 40 percent of households 
with a mortgage pay more than 30 percent 
of their monthly income on housing costs. 
Affordable and quality housing are essen-
tial components of healthy neighborhoods. 
Beyond the social, economic, and physical 
impacts on families and individuals, the con-
sequences of the lack of affordable housing 
affect everyone. For example, if teachers, 
hotel and other service workers, agricultural 
workers, nurses, and other low- to middle-in-
come workers in Oceanside must live outside 
the Planning Area, this can negatively affect 
Oceanside’s educational system, the local 
economy, access to health services, and envi-
ronmental quality. Thus, affordable rental 
and owner-occupied housing for all types of 
lower-income families and individuals should 
be of utmost importance in the General Plan 
update.


ENVIRONMENTAL JUSTICE AND PUBLIC HEALTH
9-29
• 
Understanding pesticide use and mitigat-
ing its adverse health effects. A number of 
Oceanside residents are subject to elevated 
levels of pesticides. Exposure to pesticides 
can cause short term health impacts such 
as eye and respiratory Irritation, as well as 
long term Impacts such as birth defects 
and cancer. Farmworker families and those 
who live near fields are the most likely to be 
exposed to high levels of pesticides. 
• 
A c k n o w l e d g i n g  a n d  a d d r e s s i n g 
Oceanside’s car dependence and asso-
ciated health issues. While downtown 
Oceanside is fairly walkable, the City as a 
whole is largely car-oriented has a low walk-
ability score.  Fewer Oceanside adults walk 
regularly than San Diego County residents 
as a whole. Relatively limited opportunities 
for walking may contribute to Oceanside’s 
elevated rates of childhood and adult obe-
sity. Currently, most of the Planning Area 
is not highly walkable in areas outside of 
downtown. As new, denser development 
continues to be built and the Planning Area’s 
population grows, improving walkability 
and pedestrian environments (including tree 
canopy and landscaping), as well as transit 
access and service levels is key, as is ensur-
ing that improvements are made across both 
new developments and existing communi-
ties with less access to resources.  
• 
Improving access to grocery stores. While 
Oceanside is well-served by several large 
chain supermarkets and smaller grocery 
stores that supply fresh fruits and vegeta-
bles, most of these are only accessible by car. 
While the economic feasibility of a greater 
density of grocery stores is subject to market 
realities, such as pressure from delivery ser-
vices and other trends, policies that support 
smaller grocery stores, neighborhood mar-
kets, local food stands, and other neighbor-
hood-serving options could increase walk-
ability and healthy food accessibility for an 
increased number of Oceanside residents. 
Increasing the number of households in an 
area, particularly within the corridors, could 
also help to provide a larger market needed 
for grocery store viability. The combination 
of improved food access and more walkable, 
complete neighborhoods can help to create 
conditions that support healthy living and 
reduced rates of obesity.  
• 
Improving access to educational and 
recreational facilities, public gathering 
spaces, and scenic resources. Some of 
Oceanside’s best resources for educational 
facilities and gathering spaces are the City’s 
libraries. One of the Library’s strategic goals 
is to connect customers to a wide variety 
of programs, services and activities spon-
sored by both the Library and by other 
community agencies and organizations. In 
addition to improving programmatic access 
to resources, the City can focus on improv-
ing connections to resources throughout 
the City. This can mean both ensuring that 
there are enough facilities to serve all areas 
of the City, and improving pedestrian, bicy-
cle and transit connections to the existing 
facilities across the City. As described in 
the Mobility Report, improving bicycle and 
pedestrian comfort and connections are key 
to improving access to the City’s facilities and 
resources.
